On the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture for unitary Shimura varieties with maximal parahoric level structure at unramified primes
Sungyoon Cho, Qiao He, Zhiyu Zhang

Abstract
. In this article, we prove that a version of Tate conjectures for certain Deligne-Lusztig varieties implies the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture for unitary Shimura varieties with maximal parahoric level at unramified primes. Furthermore, we prove that the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture holds unconditionally for several new cases in any dimension.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Introduction

1.1.1.
Background

The classical
Siegel–Weil formula
(
[
25
,
26
,
29
]
) relates special
values
of certain Eisenstein series as theta functions, which are generating series of representation numbers of quadratic forms. Later on, Kudla (
[
15
,
16
]
) proposed an influential program and introduced analogues of theta series in arithmetic geometry. One of the goals of the program is to prove the so-called
arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula
relating the
central derivative
of certain Eisenstein series with a certain arithmetic analogue of theta functions, which is a generating series of arithmetic intersection numbers of
n
special divisors on Shimura varieties associated to
\mathrm{SO}(n-1,2)
or
\mathrm{U}(n-1,1)
.

For
\mathrm{U}(n-1,1)
-Shimura varieties, Kudla and Rapoport (
[
13
]
) formulated a conjectural
local arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula
at an
unramified
place with hyperspecial level, now known as the
Kudla–Rapoport conjecture
. As a local analogue of the arithmetic Siegel-Weil formula, it relates the
central derivative
of local densities of hermitian forms with the arithmetic intersection number of special cycles on unitary Rapoport–Zink spaces. Now this conjectural identity is also known as the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture and was recently proved by Li and Zhang in
[
19
]
. We refer the readers to the introduction of
[
19
]
for more backgrounds and related results.

One of the distinguished features of the hyperspecial case
[
13
]
is that the corresponding Rapoport-Zink space has good reduction. Accordingly, the analytic side has a clear formulation. A natural and important question is to formulate and prove analogues of the Kudla–Rapoport conjecture when the level structure is non-trivial, where many unexpected new phenomenons occur.

At a ramified place,
there are two well-studied unitary Rapoport–Zink spaces with different level structures. One of them is the
exotic smooth model
which has good reduction, and the other one is the
Krämer model
which has bad (semistable) reduction. The analogue of Kudla–Rapoport conjecture for the even dimensional exotic smooth model was formulated and proved by Li and Liu in
[
18
]
using a strategy similar to
[
19
]
. For the Krämer model, corresponding to the bad reduction in this case, the analytic side is more involved. In fact, even the formulation of the conjecture is not clear and needs to be modified. This phenomenon in the presence of bad reduction was first discovered by Kudla and Rapoport in
[
12
]
via explicit computation in their study of the Drinfeld
p
-adic half plane. A similar computation was also done in
[
24
,
8
]
for unitary special cycles on the Krämer models of the Drinfeld
p
-adic half plane. The Kudla–Rapoport conjecture for Krämer models, in general, was formulated in
[
9
]
with conceptual formulation for the modification and proved in
[
7
]
.

The present paper focus on Kudla–Rapoport conjectures with maximal parahoric level structures at an unramified prime, where more new phenomenons occur. If the level structure is almost self-dual, a Kudla-Rapoport type formula was obtained in
[
24
]
when
n=2
by explicit computation and established in general in
[
19
]
by relating it with the hyperspecial case. At an unramified prime with general maximal parahoric level structure, such formulation was first given in
[
4
]
in terms of weighted local density taking advantage of a duality between two Rapoport-Zink spaces (see §
7
). In this paper, we also give another formulation in the spirit of
[
9
,
7
]
when the intersection number involved is purely contributed by
\mathcal{Z}
-cycles.

Assuming a version of Tate conjectures for certain Deligne-Lusztig varieties, the present paper settles this conjecture for any
n
and any maximal parahoric level structures. We are able to prove the conjecture unconditionally for several special level structures for any
n
. In particular, we will give a new proof of the almost self-dual case first proved in
[
19
]
. The main results we obtained should be useful to relax the local assumptions in the arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula for
\mathrm{U}(n-1,1)
–Shimura varieties by allowing maximal parahoric levels at unramified primes. Also, it may be applied to relax the local assumptions in the arithmetic inner product formula in
[
17
,
18
]
and the
p
-adic arithmetic inner product formula by Disegni and Liu in
[
6
]
.

1.1.2.
Kudla–Rapoport conjecture

Let
p
be an odd prime. Let
F_{0}
be a finite unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with residue field
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
F
be an unramified quadratic extension of
F_{0}
. Let
\pi
be a uniformizer of both
F
and
F_{0}
. Let
\breve{F}
be the completion of the maximal unramified extension of
F
. Let
O_{F},{O_{\breve{F}}}
be the ring of integers of
F,{\breve{F}}
respectively.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer. To define the unitary Rapoport–Zink space with maximal parahoric level structure, we fix a supersingular hermitian
O_{F}
-modules
\mathbb{X}
of signature
(1,n-1)
over
\bar{k}
. The Rapoport-Zink space
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
is the formal scheme over
\mathrm{Spf}\,O_{\breve{F}}
parameterizing hermitian formal
O_{F}
-modules
X
of signature
(1,n-1)
and type
h
(see Definition
2.1
) within the quasi-isogeny class of
\mathbb{X}
. The space
\mathcal{N}
is locally of finite type, and semistable of relative dimension
n-1
over
\mathrm{Spf}\,{O_{\breve{F}}}
.

Let
\overline{\mathbb{E}}
be the framing hermitian
O_{F}
-modules of signature
(0,1)
over
\bar{k}
. We define
space of quasi-homomorphisms
to be
\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{V}_{n}\coloneqq\Hom_{O_{F}}(\overline{\mathbb{E}},\mathbb{X})\otimes_{O_{F}}F
. We can associate
\mathbb{V}
with a natural
F/F_{0}
-hermitian form to make
\mathbb{V}
a non-degenerate
F/F_{0}
-hermitian space of dimension
n
. For any subset
L\subset\mathbb{V}
, we define the
special cycle

\mathcal{Z}(L)
(resp.
\mathcal{Y}(L)
) (see §
2.2
) to be the deformation locus of
L
(resp.
\lambda\circ L
) in
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
.

Given an
O_{F}
-lattice
L\subset\mathbb{V}
of full rank
n
, we can define two integers: the
arithmetic intersection number

\Int(L)
and the
modified derived local density

\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be an
O_{F}
-lattice and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be an
O_{F}
-basis of
L
. We define the
arithmetic intersection number

(1.1)

\mathrm{Int}_{n,h}(L)\coloneqq\chi(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x_{1})}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\cdots\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x_{n})})\in\mathbb{Z},

where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x_{i})}
denotes the structure sheaf of the special divisor
\mathcal{Z}(x_{i})
,
\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}
denotes the derived tensor product of coherent sheaves on
\mathcal{N}
, and
\chi
denotes the Euler–Poincaré characteristic. By Proposition
2.12
,
\mathrm{Int}_{n,h}(L)
is independent of the choice of the basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
and hence is a well-defined invariant of
L
itself.

To define the
modified derived local density

\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
, we need to introduce local densities first. Let
M
be another hermitian
O_{F}
-lattice (of arbitrary rank) and
\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M}
denote the
O_{F_{0}}
-scheme of hermitian
O_{F}
-module homomorphisms from
L
to
M
. Then we define the corresponding
local density
to be

\mathrm{Den}(M,L)\coloneqq\lim_{d\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{|\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M}(O_{F_{0}}/\pi^{d})|}{q^{d\cdot d_{L,M}}},

where
d_{L,M}
is the dimension of
\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M}\otimes_{O_{F_{0}}}F_{0}
. Let
I_{k}
be an unimodular hermitian
O_{F}
-lattice of rank
k
. It is well-known that there exists a
local density polynomial

\mathrm{Den}(M,L,X)\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
such that for any integer
k\geq 0
,

(1.2)

\mathrm{Den}(M,L,(-q)^{-k})=\mathrm{Den}(I_{k}\obot M,L).

Here
I_{k}\obot M
denotes the orthogonal direct sum of
I_{k}
and
M
.

When
M
also has rank
n
and
M\otimes_{O_{F}}F
is not isometric to
L\otimes_{O_{F}}F
, we have
\mathrm{Den}(M,L)=0
. In this case we write

\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(M,L)\coloneqq-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}X}\bigg|_{X=1}\mathrm{Den}(M,L,X),

and define the (normalized)
derived local density

(1.3)

\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(I_{n,h},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n,h},I_{n,h})}\in\mathbb{Q}.

Here
I_{n,h}
is a hermitian lattice with moment matrix
\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h},(\pi)^{h})
. When the
n
and
h
are clear in the context, we also simply denote it as
\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(L)
.

Then the naive analogue of the Kudla–Rapoport conjecture for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
should be the identity

\mathrm{Int}_{n,h}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L).

However, as we mentioned before, since
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
has bad reduction, some modification for the analytic side is needed. Indeed, a similar consideration as in
[
9
,
7
]
shows that naive analogue cannot be true for trivial reasons. For example, if
h>0
, then
\Int(L)
vanishes by definition if
L
has
I_{n-h+1}
as a direct summand while
\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(L)
does not vanish for such
L
by some direct computation. We call an integral
O_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{V}
a
vertex lattice of type
t
if
\Lambda^{\vee}/\Lambda
is a
k
-vector space of dimension
t
. In particular, for a vertex lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{V}
of type
t<h
, we have
\Int(\Lambda)=0
, while
\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(\Lambda)\neq 0
in general.

In order to have
\Int(\Lambda_{t})=\partial\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t})
for vertex lattice
\Lambda_{t}
of type
t<h
, we define
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
by modifying
\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(L)
with a linear combination of the (normalized)
local densities

(1.4)

\mathrm{Den}_{n,t}(L)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t},\Lambda_{t})}\in\mathbb{Z}.

In fact
\mathbb{V}\not\approx I_{n}^{[h]}\otimes_{O_{F}}F
. As a result, if
\Lambda_{t}\subset\mathbb{V}
, then
t
and
h
have different parity.

Definition 1.2

(Definition
3.2
)
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be an
O_{F}
-lattice. Define the
modified derived local density

(1.5)

\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(L)\coloneqq\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L)+\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor\frac{(h-1)}{2}\rfloor}c_{n,h-1-2i}\cdot\mathrm{Den}_{n,h-1-2i}(L).

The coefficients
c_{n,i}\in\mathbb{Q}
here are chosen to satisfy

(1.6)

\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(\Lambda_{i})=0,\quad\text{ for }0\leq i\leq h-1\text{ and }i\equiv h-1\mod{2},

which turns out to be a linear system in
(c_{n,i})
with a unique solution.

Finally, we propose the following Kudla-Rapoport conjecture for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
.

Conjecture 1.3

(Conjecture
3.3
, Conjecture
7.7
, Conjecture
8.8
)
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be an
O_{F}
-lattice. Then we have

\mathrm{Int}_{n,h}(L)=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(L).

Remark 1.4
.

We remark that
\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L)
is not an integer in general. Only the modified
\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(L)
is an integer. However, a priori, this is not clear at all.

The main purpose of this paper is to prove Conjecture
1.3
assuming a version of Tate conjectures for certain Deligne-Lusztig varieties.

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem
11.4
)
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be an
O_{F}
-lattice of rank
n
. Assuming Conjecture
6.3
, we have

\mathrm{Int}_{n,h}(L)=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(L).

In this paper, we also verified Conjecture
6.3
for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[1]}
,
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-1]}
and
\mathcal{N}_{4}^{[2]}
unconditionally.

Theorem 1.6

(Theorem
6.5
, Theorem
11.5
)
.

Assume
(n,h)
is one of the following cases:
(n,1),(n,n-1)
and
(4,2)
. Then Conjecture
1.3
holds unconditionally.

Remark 1.7
.

Conjecture
1.3
for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
was the original Kudla-Rapoport conjecture proposed in
[
13
]
and proved in
[
19
]
. The case
(n,h)=(n,1)
was first proved by
[
19
]
using certain Hecke correspondence that relates
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[1]}
with
\mathcal{N}_{n+1}
. Our proof is an attempt to prove the general case uniformly in a way closer to the proof for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[0]}
in
[
19
]
.

Remark 1.8
.

Although
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[1]}\cong\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-1]}
by a natural duality,
\mathcal{Z}
-cycles on
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[1]}
will be transformed into
\mathcal{Y}
-cycles on
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-1]}
. Hence, Theorem
1.6
for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-1]}
is different with the case for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[1]}
. In particular, Theorem
1.6
for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-1]}
and
\mathcal{N}_{4}^{[2]}
are new.

We remark that Conjecture
1.3
is based on a different viewpoint from the one formulated in
[
4
]
. The conjecture formulated in
[
4
]
is inspired by the duality
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}\cong\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-h]}
and is more general in the sense that it also considers the case when the intersection is between
\mathcal{Z}
-cycles and
\mathcal{Y}
-cycles. However, since our main theorem in this paper is about intersections between
\mathcal{Z}
-cycles and the above formulation is closer to previously studied cases e.g.
[
19
]
,
[
18
]
,
[
20
]
,
[
7
]
, we state this new formulation in the introduction. We refer the reader to Conjecture
7.6
for the general conjecture formulated in
[
4
]
and Conjecture
7.7
for the specialized conjecture about the intersection between
\mathcal{Z}
-cycles. We show that conjectures
1.3
and
7.7
are in fact equivalent in Proposition
8.9
, which is interestingly a nontrivial fact to prove.

1.1.3.
Strategy and novelty

Our general strategy is similar to the strategy of
[
19
]
using the local modularity and uncertainty principle. More precisely, fix an
O_{F}
-lattice
L^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
of rank
n-1
and consider functions on
\mathbb{V}\setminus L^{\flat}_{F}
,

\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat}}(x)\coloneqq\Int(L^{\flat}+\langle x\rangle),\quad\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat}}(x)\coloneqq\partial\mathrm{Den}(L^{\flat}+\langle x\rangle).

We need to show
\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat}}=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat}}
as functions on
\mathbb{V}\setminus L_{F}^{\flat}
. First, we have a natural decomposition of
\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat}}
and
\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat}}
into horizontal parts and vertical parts:

\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat}}=\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}+\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}},\quad\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat}}=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}+\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}.

For the horizontal parts, we have
\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{H}}
by direct comparison. For the vertical parts,
\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}
and
\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}
satisfy “
local modularity
” in the sense that their Fourier-transforms have nice behaviors.

Due to the existence of nontrivial levels, new phenomenons have already shown up for the horizontal part. We can decompose the horizontal cycles into primitive horizontal cycles indexed by “horizontal lattices” and essentially reduce the computation to
n=2
, similar to
[
19
]
. However, first of all, we have two different types of the “horizontal lattices” indexing the primitive horizontal cycle. Moreover, the primitive piece indexed by one of them admits a further decomposition into mixed special cycles. Here mixed special cycles mean that they are intersections between both
\mathcal{Z}
-cycles and
\mathcal{Y}
-cycles. We refer the readers to Theorem
5.3
for more details.

Now we turn our attention to the vertical part.
We discuss the geometric side first. For a curve
C\subset\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
, let
\mathrm{Int}_{C}(\mathcal{Z}(x))\coloneqq\chi(\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]},\mathcal{O}_{C}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)})
and
\mathrm{Int}_{C}(\mathcal{Y}(x))\coloneqq\chi(\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]},\mathcal{O}_{C}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}(x)})
. If
C
lies in
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{[1]}
or
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{[0]}
and is embedded into
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
via an embedding of
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{[1]}
or
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{[0]}
into
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
, then explicit computation shows that

(1.7)

\displaystyle \widehat{\Int}_{C}(\mathcal{Z}(x))=-q^{-h}\cdot\mathrm{Int}_{C}(\mathcal{Y}(x)),

where
\widehat{\Int}_{C}(\mathcal{Z}(x))
denotes the Fourier transform of
\mathrm{Int}_{C}(\mathcal{Z}(x))
(see §
1.2
for more details). We call such a curve
C
a special curve. This was first observed in
[
19
]
when
h=0
. In fact, when
h=0
, the more general identity

\widehat{\Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(\mathcal{Z}(x))=-\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(\mathcal{Y}(x))

was proved in
[
19
]
, which we call
local modularity
in this case. When
h\geq 0
and
C
is special, (
1.7
) was observed in
[
30
]
based on computation of intersection numbers on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{[1]}
established in
[
24
]
. In
[
30
]
, this local modularity was used to prove arithmetic transfer conjecture in a similar setting.

Nevertheless, it is by no means clear that this will be true in general. One observation is that if
\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)
can be written as a linear combination of
\mathrm{Int}_{C}(x)
for special
C
, then we have

(1.8)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)``="\begin{cases}-q^{-h}\cdot\mathrm{Int}_{n-1,h-1,\mathscr{V}}(L^{\flat})&\text{ if ${\mathrm{val}}(x)=-1$},\\
0&\text{ if ${\mathrm{val}}(x)\leq-2$}.\end{cases}

Although it is not clear whether
\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)
can be written as a linear combination of
\mathrm{Int}_{C}(x)
for special
C
, one can always try to test the corresponding speculation on the analytic side, which is a central idea for many of the past works. Indeed, for example, we recognized that there should exist two different types of horizontal lattices by testing the analytic side first (the horizontal part goes to infinity when
{\mathrm{val}}(x)
goes to infinity). Also, it is really the computation of
\widehat{\partial\mathrm{Den}}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)
in the
\mathcal{N}_{4}^{[2]}
case inspires us what we should expect in general for the geometric side as we explained in §
4
.

On the other hand, once we have a big picture for the geometric side with the help of explicit computation from the analytic side, the insight from the geometric side also serves as a guiding principle to obtain purely analytic results. For example,
[
7
, Proposition 7.5]
is inspired by the fact that
\mathcal{Z}(L)
is empty for non-integral
L
, and the proof of
[
7
, Proposition 7.5]
gives important hints for how to prove the rest major analytic results of
[
7
]
. This is also what happened in the current case.

Indeed, as one of the main results, we manage to prove an analytic analogue of (
1.8
) unconditionally. More precisely, we prove
\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)
can be extended to
\mathbb{V}
as a locally constant function and

(1.9)

\displaystyle\widehat{\partial\mathrm{Den}}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)=\begin{cases} -q^{-h}\cdot\mathrm{Int}_{n-1,h-1,\mathscr{V}}(L^{\flat})&\text{ if ${\mathrm{val}}(x)=-1$},\\
0&\text{ if ${\mathrm{val}}(x)\leq-2$}.\end{cases}

As a result, assuming (
1.8
), for any
x
with
{\mathrm{val}}(x)<0
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\Int}_{L^{\flat}}(x)-\widehat{\partial\mathrm{Den}}_{L^{\flat}}(x)=\widehat{\Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)-\widehat{\partial\mathrm{Den}}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)=0.

Then the identity
\Int_{L^{\flat}}(x)-\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat}}(x)=0
follows from the uncertainty principle as in
[
19
]
.

We remark that (
1.9
) suggests that
\mathrm{Int}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)
might be written as a linear combination of
\mathrm{Int}_{C}(x)
for special curve
C
. When
h=0
, this is indeed the case and was proved in
[
19
, Corollary 5.3.3]
. Therefore we propose this statement as a conjecture in Conjecture
6.3
.

In order to establish (
1.9
), we make use of the
primitive decomposition
of the local density polynomial into primitive local density polynomials and obtain a decomposition of
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
:

(1.10)

\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}}\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L^{\prime}),

where
L^{\prime}
runs over
O_{F}
-lattices in
L_{F}
containing
L
, and the symbol
\mathrm{Pden}
stands for the primitive version of
\mathrm{Den}
(see (
3.6
)). The key reason to consider this primitive decomposition and
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
is that we usually have a very simple formula for
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
which eventually makes the computation about
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
possible. This is the case for all the previously proved Kudla-Rapoport type formulas, e.g.
[
19
]
,
[
18
]
,
[
20
]
, and
[
7
]
.

Although a similar approach to compute
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
as in
[
7
]
may be directly generalized to the current situation, we decide to generalize the main result of
[
5
]
to obtain an explicit formula of
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
(see Proposition
8.17
). One of the reasons we choose this approach is due to the fact that Proposition
8.17
itself is already a certain induction formula that reduces the computation of
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
to the good reduction case (see Remark
8.18
), which is particularly handy in order to prove Theorem
1.9
below.

One of the major new phenomenons and difficulties we found and overcame in this paper is the fact that when there is a non-trivial level structure,
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)

no longer
has a simple formula in general. This can be seen via some explicit computation using Proposition
8.17
.
Nevertheless, inspired by an attempt to compute the Fourier transform of
\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)
(e.g. to obtain (
1.9
)), we find simple inductive formulas for
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
which suffices to control the Fourier transform of
\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L^{\flat},\mathscr{V}}(x)
.
In fact, such inductive formulas also hold in all the previously studied cases and the simple formulas for
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
in these cases follow as a direct corollary.

More precisely, let
t_{i}(L)
and
t_{\geq i}(L)
be the number of the fundamental invariants of
L
that is exactly
i
and at least
i
respectively. Then we have the following.

Theorem 1.9

(Theorem
9.4
)
.

Let
L
be a hermitian lattice with
{\mathrm{val}}(L)\not\equiv h\mod 2
. We have
\partial\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}(L)
depends only on
(t_{\geq 2}(L),t_{1}(L),t_{0}(L))
. For simplicity, we denote it as
D_{n,h}(t_{2},t_{1},t_{0})
. Moreover, assume that
(t_{2}-1,t_{1}+1,t_{0})\neq(n-h,h,0)
,
t_{0}\leq n-h
and
t_{2}\geq 1
, then we have

\displaystyle D_{n,h}(t_{2},t_{1},t_{0})-D_{n,h}(t_{2}-1,t_{1}+1,t_{0})=-(-q)^{2n-h-1-t_{1}-2t_{0}}D_{n-1,h-1}(t_{2}-1,t_{1},t_{0}).

The above inductive formula is complemented by the following simple formulas for special
D_{n,h}(t_{2},t_{1},t_{0})
.

Theorem 1.10
.

With the same notations and assumptions as in Theorem
1.9
, we have the following.

(1)

(Lemma
9.5
, Proposition
9.6
) If
t_{0}>n-h
, then
D_{n,h}(t_{2},t_{1},t_{0})=0
.

(2)

(Theorem
9.8
(1)) If
t_{2}=0
and
h+1\leq t_{1}
. Then, we have

D_{n,h}(0,t_{1},t_{0})=\dfrac{\prod_{l=h+1}^{t_{1}}(1-(-q)^{l})}{(1-(-q)^{t_{1}-h})}.

(3)

(Theorem
9.8
(2)) If
t_{2}=1
and
h-1\leq t_{1}
. Then, we have

D_{n,h}(1,t_{1},t_{0})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{ if }t_{1}=h-1,h;\\
\prod_{l=h+1}^{t_{1}}(1-(-q)^{l})&\text{ if }t_{1}\geq h+1.\end{array}\right.

Theorems
1.9
and
1.10
will be proved in §
9
using the formula for
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
obtained in
[
5
]
. As we have remarked, the method developed in
[
7
]
may also be adapted to the current case. However, we find that using the formulas in
[
5
]
is easier for our purpose so we stick with this approach. Note that the formulas in
[
5
]
are derived via a duality between weighted local densities in analogy with the duality between
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
and
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-h]}
, and this might be a particular reason for why this formula so applicable in the current case.

With the help of formulas for
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
, we finally prove (
1.9
) via certain involved weighted lattice counting in §
10
via a similar method as in
[
20
,
7
]
. However, since
\partial\mathrm{Pden}(L)
now depends on
t_{\geq 2}(L),t_{1}(L)
and
t_{0}(L)
, the counting becomes much more involved compared with the ones in
[
20
,
7
]
.

1.2.
Notation and terminology

•

Let
p
be an odd prime. Let
F_{0}
be a finite unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with residue field
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
F
be the unramified quadratic extension of
F_{0}
. Let
\pi
be a uniformizer of
F
and
F_{0}
. Let
\breve{F}
be the completion of the maximal unramified extension of
F
. Let
O_{F},{O_{\breve{F}}}
be the ring of integers of
F,{\breve{F}}
respectively.

•

We say a sublattice of a hermitian space is non-degenerate if the restriction of the hermitian form to it is non-degenerate.

•

In this paper, without explicit mentioning a lattice means a non-degenerate hermitian
O_{F}
-lattice. Unless otherwise stated, the symbol
L
always means a non-degenerate lattice of rank
n
with a hermitian form
(\,,\,)
.

•

We define
L^{\vee}
to be the dual lattice of
L
with respect to the hermitian form
(\,,\,)
. If
L\subset L^{\vee}
, we say
L
is integral. If
L\subset L^{\vee}\subset\pi^{-1}L
, we say
L
is a vertex lattice.

•

We say that a basis
\left\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}\right\}
of
L
is a normal basis (which always exists) if its moment matrix
T=((\ell_{i},\ell_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Diag}(\pi^{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,\pi^{\alpha_{n}})

where
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover, we define its fundamental invariants
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
to be the unique nondecreasing rearrangement of
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
.

•

We define the valuation of
L
to be
\mathrm{val}(L)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}
, where
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
are the fundamental invariants of
L
. For
x\in L
, we define
{\mathrm{val}}(x)={\mathrm{val}}((x,x))
, where
{\mathrm{val}}(\pi)=1
.

•

We call a sublattice
N\subset M
primitive in
M
if
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\overline{N}=r(N)
, where
\overline{N}=(N+\pi M)/\pi M
. We also use
\overline{L}
to denote
L\otimes_{O_{F}}O_{F}/(\pi).

•

We use
I_{m}
to denote a unimodular lattice of rank
m
, and
I_{n,h}
to denote a hermitian lattice with moment matrix
\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h},(\pi)^{h})
.

•

For a hermitian space
\mathbb{V}
, we let
\mathbb{V}^{?i}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{V}\mid{\mathrm{val}}(x)?i\}
where
?
can be
\geq
,
\leq
or
=
.

•

Fix an unramified additive character
\psi:F_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Here ”unramifiedness” means that the conductor of
\psi
(i.e., the largest fractional ideal in
F_{0}
on which
\psi
is trivial) is
O_{F_{0}}
. For an integrable function
f
on
\mathbb{V}
, we define its Fourier transform
\widehat{f}
to be

\widehat{f}(x)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{V}}f(y)\psi(\operatorname{tr}_{F/F_{0}}(x,y))\mathrm{d}y,\quad x\in\mathbb{V}.

We normalize the Haar measure on
\mathbb{V}
to be self-dual, so
\hat{f}(x)=f(-x)
. For an
O_{F}
-lattice
L\subset\mathbb{V}
of rank
n
, we have (under the assumption that
F/F_{0}
is unramified)

\widehat{\mathbf{1}}_{L}=\operatorname{vol}(L)\mathbf{1}_{L^{\vee}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{vol}(L)=\left[L^{\vee}:L\right]^{-1/2}=q^{-\operatorname{{\mathrm{val}}}(L)}.

Note that
\operatorname{{\mathrm{val}}}(L)
can be defined for any lattice
L
(not necessarily integral) so that the above equality for
\operatorname{vol}(L)
holds.

1.3.
Acknowledgement

We would like to thank Sungmun Cho, Benjamin Howard, Chao Li, Yifeng Liu, Michael Rapoport, Tonghai Yang and Wei Zhang for helpful discussions or comments. Part of the work was done while Q. H. and Z. Z. were participating in the “Algebraic Cycles,
L
-values, and Euler Systems” program held in MSRI during Spring 2023. We would like to thank MSRI for the excellent work conditions, financial support, and hospitality. S.C. was supported by POSTECH Basic Science Research Institute under the NRF grant number NRF2021R1A6A1A1004294412.

2.
Rapoport-Zink space and special cycles

2.1.
Rapoport-Zink space

In this section, we review the definition and basic properties of Rapoport-Zink space and special cycles.

Definition 2.1
.

For any
\mathrm{Spf}\,O_{\breve{F}}
-scheme
S
,
a hermitian formal
O_{F}
-moodule

(X,\iota,\lambda)
of signature
(1,n-1)
and type
h
over
S
is the following data:

•

X
is a strict formal
O_{F_{0}}
-module over
S
of relative height
2n
and dimension
n
. Strictness means the induced action of
O_{F_{0}}
on
\Lie X
is via the structure morphism
O_{F_{0}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
.

•

\iota:O_{F}\to\End(X)
is an action of
O_{F}
on
X
that extends the action of
O_{F_{0}}
. We require that the
Kottwitz condition
of signature
(1,n-1)
holds for all
a\in O_{F}
:

(2.1)

\mathrm{char}(\iota(a)\mid\Lie X)=(T-a)(T-\overline{a})^{n-1}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[T].

•

\lambda
is a polarization on
X
, which is
O_{F}/O_{F_{0}}
semi-linear in the sense that the Rosati involution
\mathrm{Ros}_{\lambda}
induces the non-hrivial involution on
\iota:O_{F}\to\End(X)
.

•

We require that the finite flat group scheme
\mathrm{Ker}\,\lambda
over
S
lies in
X[\pi]
and is of order
q^{2h}
.

An isomorphism
(X_{1},\iota_{1},\lambda_{1})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(X_{2},\iota_{2},\lambda_{2})
between two such triples is an
O_{F}
-linear isomorphism
\varphi\colon X_{1}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}X_{2}
such that
\varphi^{*}(\lambda_{2})=\lambda_{1}
. Up to
O_{F}
-linear quasi-isogeny compatible with the polarization, there exists a unique such triple
({\mathbb{X}},\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}},\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}})
over
{\mathbb{F}}
. Fix one choice of
({\mathbb{X}},\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}},\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}})
as the
framing object
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let (Nilp) be the category of
O_{F}
-schemes
S
such that
\pi
is locally nilpotent on
S
. Then the Rapoport–Zink space associated with
({\mathbb{X}},\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}},\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}})
is the functor

{\mathcal{N}}_{({\mathbb{X}},\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}},\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}})}={\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[h]}\to\mathrm{Spf}\,O_{\breve{F}}

sending
S\in
(Nilp) to the set of isomorphism classes of tuples
(X,\iota,\lambda,\rho)
, where

•

(X,\iota,\lambda)
is a hermitian formal
O_{F}
-module of dimension
n
and type
h
over
S
;

•

\rho:X\times_{S}{\overline{S}}\to{\mathbb{X}}\times_{\mathbb{F}}\overline{S}
is an
O_{F}
-linear quasi-isogeny of height
0
over the reduction
\overline{S}\coloneqq S\times_{O_{\breve{F}_{0}}}\mathbb{F}
such that
\rho^{*}(\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}},\overline{S}})=\lambda_{\overline{S}}
.

The functor
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
is representable by a formal scheme over
\operatorname{Spf}O_{\breve{F}}
which is locally formally of finite type by
[
23
]
. Moreover, this formal scheme is regular (see
[
2
, Proposition 3.33]
). We often simply denote
{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[h]}
as
\mathcal{N}
if the signature and type are clear in the context.

There is an isomorphism
\theta:{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[h]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-h]}
constructed as follows (
[
2
, Remark 5.2]
). For each
S\in
(Nilp),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[h]}(S)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-h]}(S),

\displaystyle(X,i_{X},\lambda_{X},\rho_{X})

\displaystyle\mapsto(X^{\vee},\bar{i}_{X}^{\vee},\lambda_{X}^{\prime},(\rho_{X}^{\vee})^{-1}),

where
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}:X^{\vee}\rightarrow X
is the unique polarization such that
\lambda_{X}^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{X}=

i_{X}(\pi)
, and for
a\in O_{F}
, the action
\bar{i}_{X}^{\vee}
is defined as
\bar{i}_{X}^{\vee}(a)\coloneqq i_{X}(a^{*})^{\vee}
.
When we denote
{\mathcal{N}}_{n}^{[h]}
as
\mathcal{N}
, we use
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
to denote
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-h]}
.

2.2.
Special cycles

To define special cycles, we need to fix a hermitian formal
O_{F}
-moodule
(\overline{\mathbb{E}},i_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}},\lambda_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}})
of signature
(0,1)
and type
0
over
\mathbb{F}
. Then we can similarly define a Rapoport-Zink Space
\mathcal{N}_{(\overline{\mathbb{E}},i_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}},\lambda_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}})}
which we denote as
\mathcal{N}^{0}
for simplicity. Recall that there is a unique lifting
(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\iota_{\overline{\mathcal{E}}},\lambda_{\overline{\mathcal{E}}})
of
(\overline{\mathbb{E}},i_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}},\lambda_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}})
over
O_{\breve{F}}
.

Definition 2.3
.

We define the space of special homomorphism to be the
F
-vector space

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\coloneqq\Hom_{O_{F}}(\overline{{\mathbb{E}}},\mathbb{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{Q}}.

We can associate
\mathbb{V}
with a naturally defined hermitian form as follows. For
x,y\in\mathbb{V}
, we define a hermitian form
h
on
\mathbb{V}
as

(x,y)=\lambda_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}^{-1}\circ y^{\vee}\circ\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}\circ x\in\operatorname{End}_{O_{F}}(\overline{\mathbb{E}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{\mathbb{\mathbb{X}}}^{\simeq}}}{{\simeq}}F.

We often omit
i_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}^{-1}
via the identification
\operatorname{End}_{O_{F}}(\overline{\mathbb{E}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\simeq F
.

Definition 2.4
.

[
13
, Definition 3.2]
,
[
2
, Definition 5.4]

(1)

For
x\in\mathbb{V}
, we define the special cycle
\mathcal{Z}(x)
to be the closed formal subscheme of
\mathcal{N}^{0}\times\mathcal{N}
with the following property: For each
O_{F}
-scheme
S
such that
\pi
is locally nilpotent,
\mathcal{Z}(x)(S)
is the set of all points
\xi=(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S},\iota_{\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S}},\lambda_{\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S}},X,i_{X},\lambda_{X},\rho_{X})
in
\mathcal{N}^{0}\times\mathcal{N})(S)
such that the quasi-homomorphism

\rho_{X}^{-1}\circ x\circ\rho_{\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S}}:\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S}\times_{S}\overline{S}\rightarrow X\times_{S}\overline{S}

extends to a homomorphism from
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
to
X
.

(2)

For
y\in\mathbb{V}
, we define the special cycle
\mathcal{Y}(y)
in
\mathcal{N}^{0}\times\mathcal{N}
as follows. First, consider the special cycle
\mathcal{Z}(\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}\circ y)
in
\mathcal{N}^{0}\times\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
. This is the closed formal subscheme of
\mathcal{N}^{0}\times\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
. We define
\mathcal{Y}(y)
as
(id\times\theta^{-1})(\mathcal{Z}(\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}\circ y))
in
\mathcal{N}^{0}\times\mathcal{N}

Note that
\mathcal{N}^{0}
can be identified with
\operatorname{Spf}O_{\breve{F}}
, and hence
\mathcal{Z}(x),\mathcal{Y}(y)
can be identified with closed formal subschemes of
\mathcal{N}
.

The same proof of
[
14
, Proposition 5.9]
gives us the following.

Proposition 2.5
.

[
14
, Proposition 5.9]
The functors
\mathcal{Z}(x)
and
\mathcal{Y}(y)
are representable by Cartier divisors of
\mathcal{N}
.

Special cycles have the following properties.

Proposition 2.6
.

[
2
, Proposition 5.10]
Let
x,y\in{\mathbb{V}}
.

(1)

If
{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))=0
, then
{\mathcal{Z}}(x)\simeq{\mathcal{N}}^{[h]}_{n-1}
.

(2)

If
{\mathrm{val}}((y,y))=-1
, then
{\mathcal{Y}}(y)\simeq{\mathcal{N}}^{[h-1]}_{n-1}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

[
2
, Proposition 5.11]

Assume that
{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))=0
and
{\mathrm{val}}((y,y))=-1
. Assume further that by rescaling as in the proof of
[
2
, Proposition 5.10]
,
x^{*}\circ x=1
, and
(\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}}\circ y)^{*}\circ(\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}}\circ y)=1
. Here,
x^{*}
(resp.
(\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}}\circ y)^{*}
) is the adjoint of
x
(resp.
(\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}}\circ y)
) with respect to the polarizations
\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}}
and
\lambda_{\overline{{\mathbb{E}}}}
(resp.
\lambda^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{X}}}
and
\lambda_{\overline{{\mathbb{E}}}}
). We define
e_{x}\coloneqq x\circ x^{*}
and
e_{y}\coloneqq(\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}}\circ y)\circ(\lambda_{{\mathbb{X}}}\circ y)^{*}
. Fix isomorphisms

\begin{split}\Phi:{\mathcal{Z}}(x)\simeq{\mathcal{N}}^{[h]}_{n-1},\\
\Psi:{\mathcal{Y}}(y)\simeq{\mathcal{N}}^{[h-1]}_{n-1},\end{split}

as in Proposition
2.6
. Then the following statements hold.

(1)

For
z\in{\mathbb{V}}
such that
(x,z)=0
, let
z^{\prime}\coloneqq(1-e_{x})\circ z
. Then, we have
\Phi({\mathcal{Z}}(x)\cap{\mathcal{Z}}(z))={\mathcal{Z}}(z^{\prime})
in
{\mathcal{N}}^{[h]}_{n-1}
and
(z^{\prime},z^{\prime})=(z,z)
.

(2)

For
w\in{\mathbb{V}}
such that
(x,w)=0
, let
w^{\prime}\coloneqq(1-e_{x})\circ w
. Then, we have
\Phi({\mathcal{Z}}(x)\cap{\mathcal{Y}}(w))={\mathcal{Y}}(w^{\prime})
in
{\mathcal{N}}^{[h]}_{n-1}
and
(w^{\prime},w^{\prime})=(w,w)
.

(3)

For
z\in{\mathbb{V}}
such that
(y,z)=0
, let
z^{\prime}\coloneqq(1-e_{y}^{\vee})\circ z
. Then, we have
\Psi({\mathcal{Y}}(y)\cap{\mathcal{Z}}(z))={\mathcal{Z}}(z^{\prime})
in
{\mathcal{N}}^{[h-1]}_{n-1}
and
(z^{\prime},z^{\prime})=(z,z)
.

(4)

For
w\in{\mathbb{V}}
such that
(y,w)=0
, let
w^{\prime}\coloneqq(1-e_{y}^{\vee})\circ w
. Then, we have
\Psi({\mathcal{Y}}(y)\cap{\mathcal{Y}}(w))={\mathcal{Y}}(w^{\prime})
in
{\mathcal{N}}^{[h-1]}_{n-1}
and
(w^{\prime},w^{\prime})=(w,w)
.

2.3.
Horizontal and vertical part of special cycles

We closely follow
[
19
, §2.9]
in this subsection.
We call a formal scheme
Z
over
\operatorname{Spf}

O_{\breve{F}}
vertical (resp. horizontal) if
\pi
is locally nilpotent on
Z
(resp. flat over
\operatorname{Spf}O_{\breve{F}}
). In particular, the formal scheme-theoretic union of two vertical (resp. horizontal) formal subschemes of a formal scheme is again vertical (resp. horizontal).

Now we define the horizontal part and vertical part of
Z
respectively. The horizontal part
Z_{\mathscr{H}}
of
Z
is defined to be the closed formal subscheme with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{Z}\left[\pi^{\infty}\right]\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
. Then
Z_{\mathscr{H}}
is the maximal horizontal closed formal subscheme of
Z
.
For noetherian
Z
, we can find
N\gg 0
such that
\pi^{N}\mathcal{O}_{Z}\left[\pi^{\infty}\right]=0
. Then the vertical part
Z_{\mathscr{V}}\subset Z
is defined to be the closed formal subscheme with ideal sheaf
\pi^{N}\mathcal{O}_{Z}
.

Note that
\mathcal{O}_{Z}\left[\pi^{\infty}\right]\cap\pi^{N}\mathcal{O}_{Z}=0
implies the following decomposition:

Z=Z_{\mathscr{H}}\cup Z_{\mathscr{V}}.

The same proof of
[
19
, Lemma 2.9.2]
gives the following.

Lemma 2.8
.

[
19
, Lemma 2.9.2]

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be a
O_{F}
-lattice of rank
r\geq n-1
such that
L_{F}
is non-degenerate. Then
\mathcal{Z}(L)
is noetherian.

The following lemma follows from the same proof of
[
19
, Lemma 5.1.1]
.

Lemma 2.9
.

[
19
, Lemma 5.1.1]

Let
L^{\flat}
be an
O_{F}
-lattice of rank
n-1
in
\mathbb{V}_{n}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}(L^{b})_{\mathscr{V}}
is supported on
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathrm{red}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}(L^{b})_{\mathscr{V}}}
is annihilated by a power of the ideal sheaf of
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{\mathrm{red}}\subset\mathcal{N}_{n}
.

2.4.
Linear invariance

Following
[
11
]
, we show the linear invariance of intersection numbers. In this subsection, we use
\mathcal{N}
to denote
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
and
(X,\iota_{X},\lambda_{X})
to denote the the universal object over
\mathcal{N}
. Let
\mathrm{D}(X)
denote the covariant Grothendieck-Messing crystal of
X
restricted to the Zariski site. Then we have a short exact sequence of locally free
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}
-modules:

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\operatorname{Fil}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{D}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{Lie}(X)\rightarrow 0.

which is
O_{F}
-linear via the action given by
\iota_{X}
. Let

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle\coloneqq\pi\otimes 1+1\otimes\pi\in O_{F}\otimes_{O_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}},

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\pi\otimes 1+1\otimes\pi\in O_{F}\otimes_{O_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
L_{X}
be the image of
\iota(\pi)+\pi
on
\mathrm{Lie}(X)
. In other words,
L_{X}\coloneqq\epsilon\Lie(X).

According to the Kottwitz signature condition, we know
L_{X}\subset\mathrm{Lie}(X)
is locally a
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}
-module direct summand of rank
1
.

For a closed formal subscheme
Z
of
\mathcal{N}
with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z}
, we denote by
\tilde{Z}
the closed formal subscheme defined by the sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z}^{2}
. Let
x\in\mathbb{V}
be a non-zero special homomorphism. Let
X_{0}
be the universal object of
{\mathcal{N}}^{[0]}_{1}
. By the very definition of
\mathcal{Z}(x)
, we have

X_{0}|_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{\rightarrow}}X|_{\mathcal{Z}(x)},

which induces an
O_{F}
-linear morphism of vector bundles

\mathrm{D}(X_{0})|_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{D}(X)|_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}.

By the Grothendieck–Messing theory, we may canonically extend the above morphism to a morphism

\mathrm{D}(X_{0})|_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{x}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{D}(X)|_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(x)},

which no longer preserves the Hodge filtrations and hence induces a nontrivial morphism

(2.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fil}(X_{0})|_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{x}}}{{\rightarrow}}\operatorname{Lie}(X)|_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(x)}.

Proposition 2.11
.

The morphism (
2.2
) induces a morphism

\operatorname{Fil}(X_{0})|_{\tilde{Z}(x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{x}}}{{\rightarrow}}L_{X}|_{\tilde{Z}(x)}.

Moreover,
Z(x)
is the vanishing locus of
\tilde{x}
.

Proof.

According to the signature condition of
X_{0}
, we have
\mathrm{Fil}(X_{0})=\epsilon\mathrm{D}(X_{0})
since both are locally
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}
direct summands of
\mathrm{D}(X_{0})
of rank
1
. Since
\tilde{x}
is
O_{F}
-linear, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{x}(\mathrm{Fil}(X_{0}))=\tilde{x}(\epsilon\mathrm{D}(X_{0}))\in\epsilon\mathrm{D}(X)=L_{X}.

Now the second claim follows from the Grothendieck–Messing theory.
∎

Now given a nonzero element
x\in\mathbb{V}
, we define a chain complex of locally free
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}
-modules

C(x)\coloneqq(\cdots\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow 0)

supported in degrees 1 and 0 with the map
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{Z}(x)}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}
being the natural inclusion. We extend the definition to
x=0
by setting

C(0)\coloneqq(\cdots\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow 0)

supported in degrees 1 and 0 , where
\omega
is the line bundle such that
\omega^{-1}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\operatorname{Fil}(X_{0}),L_{X}).

The following is our main result of this subsection which follows from Proposition
2.12
by the same argument as in
[
11
]
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
0\leqslant m\leqslant n
be an integer. Suppose that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in\mathbb{V}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\in\mathbb{V}
generate the same
O_{E}
-submodule. Then we have an isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{i}(C(x_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}C(x_{m}))\simeq\mathrm{H}_{i}(C(y_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}}C(y_{m}))

of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{N}}
-modules for every
i
.

3.
Local density and the modified Kudla–Rapoport conjecture

In this section, we discuss the conjecture
1.3
proposed in the introduction in detail.

3.1.
Local density

Let
L,M
be two integral hermitian
O_{F}
-lattices with rank
n,m
respectively. Let
\operatorname{Herm}{}_{L,M}
be the scheme of integral representations of
M
by
L
, an
O_{F_{0}}
-scheme such that for any
O_{F_{0}}
-algebra
R
,

\operatorname{Herm}_{L,M}(R)=\operatorname{Herm}(L\otimes_{O_{F_{0}}}R,M\otimes_{O_{F_{0}}}R)

where
\operatorname{Herm}
denotes the set of hermitian module homomorphisms. The local density of integral representations of
M
by
L
is defined to be

\operatorname{Den}(M,L)\coloneqq\lim_{d\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{\#\operatorname{Herm}_{L,M}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi^{d}))}{q^{d\cdot\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{Herm}_{L,M})_{F_{0}}}}.

If the generic fiber
(\operatorname{Herm}_{L,M})_{F_{0}}
is non-empty, then we have
n\leq m
and

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{Herm}_{M,L})_{F_{0}}=\operatorname{dim}\mathrm{U}_{m}-\operatorname{dim}\mathrm{U}_{m-n}=n\cdot(2m-n).

Now we consider the local density polynomial.
Let
I_{k}
be an unimodular hermitian
O_{F}
-lattice of rank
k
. It is well-known that there exists a
local density polynomial

\mathrm{Den}(M,L,X)\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
such that for any integer
k\geq 0
,

(3.1)

\mathrm{Den}(M,L,(-q)^{-k})=\mathrm{Den}(I_{k}\obot M,L).

When
M
has also rank
n
and
\chi(M)=-\chi(L)
, we have
\mathrm{Den}(M,L)=0
and in this case we write

\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(M,L)\coloneqq-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}X}\bigg|_{X=1}\mathrm{Den}(M,L,X).

Define the (normalized)
derived local density

(3.2)

\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(I_{n,h},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n,h},I_{n,h})}\in\mathbb{Q}.

Here
I_{n,h}
is a hermitian lattice with moment matrix
\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h},(\pi)^{h})
. When the
n
and
h
are clear in the context, we also simply denote it as
\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(L)
.

Recall that an integral
O_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{V}
is a
vertex lattice of type
t
if
\Lambda^{\vee}/\Lambda
is a
\kappa
-vector space of dimension
t
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume
t<h
, we have
\Int_{n,h}(\Lambda_{t})=0
.

Proof.

First, we write
\Lambda_{t}=I_{n-t}\obot J_{t}
where
J_{t}
is a lattice with moment matrix
(p)^{t}
. According to Proposition
2.6
,
\mathcal{Z}(\Lambda_{t})
in
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{Z}(I_{h-t}\obot J_{t})
in
\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[h]}
. However, for
(X,\iota,\lambda,\rho)\in\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[h]}
, we have
\mathrm{Ker}\lambda=X[\pi]
by definition of
\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[h]}
. Hence,
\lambda=\pi\lambda^{\prime}
for some principal
\lambda^{\prime}
. Assume
(X,\iota,\lambda,\rho)\in\mathcal{Z}(x)
for some
x\in\mathbb{V}
, then it is clear from the definition of
\mathcal{Z}(x)
such that
{\mathrm{val}}(x)\geq 1
. Hence, if
{\mathrm{val}}(x)=0
, then
\mathcal{Z}(x)=\emptyset
in
\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[h]}
. In particular,
\mathcal{Z}(I_{h-t}\obot J_{t})
in
\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[h]}
is empty.
∎

The naive analogue of the Kudla–Rapoport conjecture for
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
states that

\mathrm{Int}_{n,h}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L).

However, this can not be true since
\Int_{n,h}(\Lambda_{t})=0
by Lemma
3.1
, but
\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}_{n,h}(\Lambda_{t})\neq 0
.

Now a similar consideration as in
[
9
,
7
]
suggests the following. In order to have
\Int(\Lambda_{t})=\partial\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t})
for vertex lattice
\Lambda_{t}
of type
t<h
, we define
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
by modifying
\mathrm{Den}^{\prime}(L)
with a linear combination of the (normalized)
local densities

(3.3)

\mathrm{Den}_{n,t}(L)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t},\Lambda_{t})}\in\mathbb{Z}.

Note that since
F/F_{0}
is unramified, the hermitian space over
F
is determined by the parity of valuation of the hermitian form. As a result, if
\Lambda_{t}\subset\mathbb{V}
, then
t
and
h
have different parity.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be an
O_{F}
-lattice. Define the
modified derived local density

(3.4)

\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(L)\coloneqq\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L)+\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor\frac{(h-1)}{2}\rfloor}c_{n,h-1-2i}\cdot\mathrm{Den}_{n,h-1-2i}(L).

The coefficients
c_{n,i}\in\mathbb{Q}
here are chosen to satisfy

(3.5)

\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(\Lambda_{i})=0,\quad\text{ for }0\leq i\leq h-1\text{ and }i\equiv h-1\mod{2},

which turns out to be a linear system in
(c_{n,i})
with a unique solution since
\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{j},\Lambda_{i})=0
if
j>i
.

Conjecture 3.3
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
be an
O_{F}
-lattice. Then we have

\mathrm{Int}_{n,h}(L)=\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(L).

Although the definition of
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
is very explicit, the computation of
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
is a challenging task, especially when
h>0
,
I_{n,h}
and the unimodular lattice
I_{k}
lie in two different Jordan block. One way to compute
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
is to decompose
\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
into primitive pieces as we introduce now.

Similarly to the local density polynomial, we define the primitive local density polynomial
\mathrm{Pden}(M,L,X)
to be the polynomial in
\mathbb{Q}[X]
such that

(3.6)

\mathrm{Pden}(M,L,(-q)^{-k})\coloneqq\lim_{d\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{\#\operatorname{Pherm}_{L,M}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi^{d}))}{q^{d\cdot\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{Herm}_{L,M})_{F_{0}}}},

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pherm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi^{d}))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\phi\in\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M\obot H^{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi^{d}))\mid\text{ $\phi$ is primitive}\}.

Recall that
\phi\in\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M\obot I_{k}}(O_{F_{0}}/(\pi^{d}))
is primitive if
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}((\phi(L)+\pi(M\obot I_{k}))/\pi(M\obot I_{k})=n
. In particular, we have
\mathrm{Den}(M,M)=\mathrm{Pden}(M,M)
for any hermitian
O_{F}
-lattice
M
. We can also similarly define the normalized primitive local densities:

\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L)=\frac{\mathrm{Pden}^{\prime}(I_{n,h},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(I_{n,h},I_{n,h})},\quad\text{ }\quad\mathrm{Pden}_{n,t}(L)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{Pden}(\Lambda_{t},L)}{\mathrm{Den}(\Lambda_{t},\Lambda_{t})},

and

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}(L)\coloneqq\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L)+\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor\frac{(h-1)}{2}\rfloor}c_{n,h-1-2i}\cdot\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h-1-2i}(L).

The following lemma decomposes local density polynomials into a summation of primitive local density polynomials. The following is essentially due to
[
1
]
. See also
[
19
, Theorem 3.5.1]
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
M
and
L
be lattices of rank
m
and
n
. Then we have

\mathrm{Den}(M,L,X)=\sum_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}}(q^{n-m}X)^{\ell(L^{\prime}/L)}\mathrm{Pden}(M,L^{\prime},X),

where
\ell(L^{\prime}/L)=\mathrm{length}_{O_{F}}L^{\prime}/L
. Here
\mathrm{Pden}(M,L^{\prime},X)=0
for
L^{\prime}
with fundamental invariant strictly less than the smallest fundamental invariant of
M
. In particular, the summation is finite.

Conversely, we can recover primitive local density polynomials as a linear combination of local density polynomials.

Theorem 3.5
.

[
9
, Theorem 5.2]

Let
M
and
L
be lattices of rank
m
and
n
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(M,L,X)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2+i(n-m)}X^{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L\\
\ell(L^{\prime}/L)=i\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Den}(M,L^{\prime},X).

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
L
be a lattice of rank
n
. Then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}(L)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset L^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L\\
\ell(L^{\prime}/L)=i\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Den}_{n,h}(L^{\prime}).

Lemma 3.7
.

For two lattices
L
and
M
of the same rank
n
, we have

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pden}(M,L)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Den}(M,L)&\text{ if $M\cong L$},\\
0&\text{ if $M\not\cong L$}.\end{cases}

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Den}(M,L)=n(M,L)\cdot\mathrm{Den}(M,M),

where for two lattices
M,L\subset\mathbb{V}
of rank
n
,
n(M,L)=|\{L^{\prime}\subset L_{F}\mid L\subset L^{\prime},L^{\prime}\cong M\}|.

Corollary 3.8
.

Assume
L\not\cong\Lambda_{t}
for any vertex lattice
\Lambda_{t}
with
t<h
. Then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}(L)=\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}^{\prime}(L).

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
c_{n,t}
be the coefficients in (
3.5
) with even
t
and
0<t\leq t_{\max}
. Then

\displaystyle c_{n,t}=-\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}^{\prime}(\Lambda_{t}).

Proof.

On the one hand, combining Corollary
3.6
with (
3.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}(\Lambda_{t})=0.

On the other hand, by Lemma
3.7
and (
3.4
),

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}(\Lambda_{t})=\mathrm{Pden}_{n,h}^{\prime}(\Lambda_{t})+c_{n,t}.

∎

We will give another formulation of the conjecture
3.3
(conjecture
7.7
) in §
7
which is based on the duality between
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}
and
\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[n-h]}
, and is in fact more general (it takes care of the intersections between
\mathcal{Z}
-cycles and
\mathcal{Y}
-cycles). The main terms of these two formulations are the same by direct calculations. However, interestingly, it is not clear that these two formulations have the same correction terms from the definition.

4.
Our strategy:
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}

Our general strategy is closest to the unramified unitary case
[
19
]
since
{\mathcal{N}}^{[0]}_{n}\subset{\mathcal{N}}^{[h]}_{n+h}
, and has several new ingredients which are quite complicated. Therefore, in this section, we consider the case
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}
and explain our strategy. Indeed, this is the first new case we proved and almost all ideas are essentially from this case. Therefore, we believe that this section will be helpful for readers. Since we want to explain our strategy in detail, we will freely use notations from the following sections.

Let
{\mathbb{V}}
be the space of special homomorphisms (dimension
4
), and let
L^{\flat}\subset{\mathbb{V}}
be an
O_{F}
-lattice of rank 3. For any lattice
L^{\prime\flat}
such that
L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime\flat}\subset(L^{\prime\flat})^{\vee}\subset L_{F}^{\flat}
, we define the primitive part
{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})^{\circ}
of the special cycle
{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})
inductively by setting

{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})^{\circ}\coloneqq{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})-{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime\flat}\subset L^{\prime\prime\flat}\\
L^{\prime\prime\flat}\subset(L^{\prime\prime\flat})^{\vee}\subset L^{\prime\flat}_{F}\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\prime\flat})^{\circ}.

Then, for
x\in{\mathbb{V}}\backslash L_{F}^{\flat}
, the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture (Conjecture
7.6
) is equivalent to

\text{Int}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)\coloneqq\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},\text{}^{{\mathbb{L}}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})^{\circ}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Z}}(x)})={\sum}_{L^{\prime\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime\vee},L^{\prime}\cap L_{F}^{\flat}=L^{\prime\flat}}D_{4,2}(L^{\prime})1_{L^{\prime}}(x)=:\partial Den^{4,2}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x),

where
D_{4,2}(L^{\prime})
is the Cho-Yamauchi constant for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}
(Definition
8.3
).

The first step is decomposing
\text{Int}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)
and
\partial\text{Den}^{4,2}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)
into horizontal and vertical parts:

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Int}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)=\text{Int}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ},{\mathscr{H}}}(x)+\text{Int}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ},{\mathscr{V}}}(x),\\
\partial\text{Den}^{4,2}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)=\partial\text{Den}^{4,2}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ},{\mathscr{H}}}(x)+\partial\text{Den}^{4,2}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ},{\mathscr{V}}}(x).\end{array}

In the case of the good reduction
{\mathcal{N}}^{[0]}_{n}
, this decomposition is relatively simple: if
L^{\prime\flat}
has the fundamental invariants
(0,0,\dots,0,\alpha),\alpha\geq 1
, then
\text{Int}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)
and
\partial\text{Den}^{4,2}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)
are horizontal. Otherwise,
\text{Int}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)
and
\partial\text{Den}^{4,2}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ}}(x)
are vertical.

However, when
h\geq 1
, i.e., in the case of bad reduction, horizontal parts and vertical parts cannot be separated just by the fundamental invariants of
L^{\prime\flat}
. Indeed, even in
{\mathcal{N}}^{[1]}_{2}
,
{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{1})^{\circ}

(L_{1}\simeq(\pi))
is a sum of horizontal parts and vertical parts. To understand this phenomenon, we did some explicit computation on the analytic side by using
[
3
]
: for
L_{\beta\gamma\delta}\in{\mathbb{V}}
of rank 3 with fundamental invariants
(\delta,\gamma,\beta)
, and
x\in{\mathbb{V}}\backslash(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})_{F}
with
{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))=\alpha\geq\beta\geq\gamma\geq\delta
, we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\partial\text{Den}_{L_{100}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=\alpha/2,\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\,\,\ \partial\text{Den}_{L_{300}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=(q^{2}+q)\alpha/2+1-q^{2},\\
\partial\text{Den}_{L_{311}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=(q^{7}+q^{6})\alpha/2-(q^{7}-q^{5}-1),\quad\quad\quad\partial\mathrm{Den}_{L_{210}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=q^{3}-q+1,\\
\partial\text{Den}_{L_{221}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=-(q^{2}-1)(q^{2}-q+1)(q^{3}+q^{2}+q+1),\\
\partial\text{Den}_{L_{331}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=-(q^{2}-1)(q^{6}+q^{5}+q^{4}+2q^{3}+q^{2}+1),\\
\partial\text{Den}_{L_{322}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=-(q^{2}-1)(q^{2}-q+1)(q^{4}+2q^{3}+q^{2}+q+1),\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\vdots\end{array}

The most interesting thing in this computation is the fact that
\partial\text{Den}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)
does not depend on
\alpha
if
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\neq(\beta,0,0),(\beta,1,1)
. A similar phenomenon happens in the case of
{\mathcal{N}}^{[0]}_{n}
for vertical components since these are locally constant. Therefore, it is reasonable to guess that the horizontal parts of
{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\flat})
are contained in
{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})^{\circ}
s for
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)=(\beta,0,0),(\beta,1,1)
(which turns out to be true by Theorem
5.3
). Since
{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta 00})^{\circ},{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta 11})^{\circ}
have some vertical parts too, it should be handled carefully. Anyway, horizontal parts can be understood in this way, so for now, let us focus on the cases where
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\neq(\beta,0,0),(\beta,1,1)
.

Now, we guessed that
{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})^{\circ}
is purely vertical if
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\neq(\beta,0,0),(\beta,1,1)
. The next step is to understand the Fourier transforms

\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\text{Int}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ},{\mathscr{V}}}(x)\overset{guess}{=}\widehat{\text{Int}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}(x),\\
\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ},{\mathscr{V}}}^{4,2}(x)\overset{guess}{=}\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x),\end{array}

for
x\perp L_{\beta\gamma\delta}
,
{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))<0
. In the case of good reduction
{\mathcal{N}}^{[0]}_{n}
, both
\widehat{\text{Int}}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ},{\mathscr{V}}}(x)
and
\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ},{\mathscr{V}}}^{n,0}(x)
vanish when
{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))<0
, and hence

(4.1)

\widehat{\text{Int}}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ},{\mathscr{V}}}(x)-\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L^{\prime\flat\circ},{\mathscr{V}}}^{n,0}(x)=0,\quad\text{for }{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))<0.

Since (
4.1
) is the most crucial property to prove the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture inductively, we have to show that (
4.1
) holds in our cases.

In
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}
, we still have that
\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=0
if
{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))\leq-2
, but
\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)
is not zero for
{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))=-1
(see Theorem
10.16
, Theorem
10.17
, Theorem
10.18
, Theorem
10.19
, Theorem
11.2
). Indeed, we can compute that for
{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))=-1
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{444}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}+1),&\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{431}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=-\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}(q+1)(q^{3}-q+1),\\
\\
\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{440}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}(q^{2}-1),&\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{310}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=-\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}.\end{array}

What is the meaning of these numbers? This is the main obstruction when we try to prove the conjecture. Since we cannot compute the geometric side directly, it is not possible to prove the conjecture without knowing the meaning of these numbers.

Fortunately, we had a table of the Cho-Yamauchi constants
D_{3,1}(L)
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}D_{3,1}(L_{444})=-(q^{2}-1)(q^{3}+1),&D_{3,1}(L_{431})=(q+1)(q^{3}-q+1),\\
D_{3,1}(L_{440})=-(q^{2}-1),&D_{3,1}(L_{310})=1.\end{array}

Now, it is easy to see that

(4.2)

\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)=-\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}D_{3,1}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta}).

This is the most important observation in our work since this gives the following crucial ideas.

First, note that
\widehat{\partial\mathrm{Den}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)
is a certain linear sum of the Cho-Yamauchi constants
D_{4,2}(L)
for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}
and the right-hand side of (
4.2
) is the Cho-Yamauchi constants
D_{3,1}(L)
for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[1]}_{3}
. This suggests that there should be certain inductive relations among
D_{n,h}(L)
and
D_{n-1,h-1}(L)
,
\forall 0\leq h\leq n
(see Theorem
9.4
). Since the Cho-Yamauchi constants for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[h]}_{n}
are very complicated (for example,
D_{6,2}(L)=(q-1)(q+1)^{3}(q^{2}-q+1)(q^{13}-q^{12}+q^{11}+q^{10}-2q^{9}+3q^{8}-3q^{7}+q^{6}-2q^{4}+2q^{3}-2q^{2}+q-1)
for a lattice
L
with fundamental invariants
(1,1,a_{4},a_{3},a_{2},a_{1})
,
a_{i}\geq 2
), we may not be able to find these inductive relations without the above observation (
4.2
).

These inductive relations are the most important ingredients to understand the analytic side and by using them, we have a quite complete understanding on the analytic side of the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[h]}_{n}
,
\forall 0\leq h\leq n
.

Second, note that the right-hand side of (
4.2
) is the Cho-Yamauchi constant for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[1]}_{3}
, not
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{3}
. Also, note that we need a
Y
-cycle to get a reduction from
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}
to
{\mathcal{N}}^{[1]}_{3}
(see Proposition
2.6
). This means that
Y
-cycles appear when we take the Fourier transform of
\text{Int}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}(x)=\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},\text{}^{{\mathbb{L}}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ})\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Z}}(x)})
. Indeed, the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture for
{\mathcal{N}}_{3}^{[1]}
holds, therefore,

D_{3,1}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})=\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},\text{}^{{\mathbb{L}}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})^{\circ}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Y}}(x)}),\quad{\mathrm{val}}((x,x))=-1.

Therefore, (
4.2
) suggests that

\begin{array}[]{rl}\widehat{\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},\text{}^{{\mathbb{L}}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})^{\circ}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Z}}(x)})}=\widehat{\text{Int}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}(x)&\\
\overset{conjecture}{=}\widehat{\partial\text{Den}}_{L_{\beta\gamma\delta}^{\circ}}^{4,2}(x)&=-\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}D_{3,1}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})=-\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},\text{}^{{\mathbb{L}}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})^{\circ}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Y}}(x)}).\end{array}

If we can prove this relation, then we can show that (
4.1
) holds for all
{\mathrm{val}}(x,x)<0
, and we can use an inductive argument to prove the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}
.

By
[
19
, Lemma 6.3.1]
and
[
30
, Theorem 8.1]
, we know that

\widehat{\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},O_{C}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Z}}(x)})}=-\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},O_{C}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Y}}(x)}),

if
C
is a Deligne-Lusztig curve or
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Therefore, if
{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})^{\circ}
is a linear sum of Deligne-Lusztig curves or
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
(in the Grothendieck group of coherent sheaves), then we have

\widehat{\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},\text{}^{{\mathbb{L}}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})^{\circ}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Z}}(x)})}=-\dfrac{1}{q^{2}}\chi({\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4},\text{}^{{\mathbb{L}}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L_{\beta\gamma\delta})^{\circ}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}O_{{\mathcal{Y}}(x)}).

This is how we make the Conjecture
6.3
(this can be regarded as a variant of Tate conjectures for certain Deligne-Lusztig varieties), and we prove that if Conjecture
6.3
holds, then the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture holds (see Theorem
11.4
). Then, we prove that Conjecture
6.3
holds for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}
and some other cases (see Theorem
6.5
). This is how we prove the Kudla-Rapoport conjecture for
{\mathcal{N}}^{[2]}_{4}
.

5.
Horizontal parts of Kudla–Rapoport cycles

In this section, we describe the horizontal parts of special cycles following the approach of
[
19
, §4]
. Due to the existence of non-trivial level structures, there are some new phenomena.

Let
K
denote a finite extension of
\breve{F}
. Consider
z\in\mathcal{N}_{n}^{[h]}(O_{K})
which corresponds to an
O_{F}
-hermitian module
G
of signature
(1,n-1)
over
O_{K}
. Let
T_{p}(-)
denote the integral
p
-adic Tate modules and

L\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{O_{F}}(T_{p}\overline{\mathcal{E}},T_{p}G).

We can associate
L
with a hermitian form
\{x,y\}
given by

(T_{p}\overline{\mathcal{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{\rightarrow}}T_{p}G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{G}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{p}G^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle y^{\vee}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{p}\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{\overline{\mathcal{\mathcal{E}}}}^{\vee}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{p}\overline{\mathcal{E}})\in\operatorname{End}_{O_{F}}(T_{p}\overline{\mathcal{E}})\cong O_{F}.

One can check that
L
is represented by the hermitian matrix
\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h},(\pi)^{h})
.

Following
[
19
, §4]
, we consider two injective
O_{F}
-linear isometric homomorphisms

i_{K}:\operatorname{Hom}_{O_{F}}(\overline{\mathcal{E}},G)_{F}\rightarrow L_{F},

and

i_{\bar{k}}:\operatorname{Hom}_{O_{F}}(\overline{\mathcal{E}},G)_{F}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}.

By
[
19
, Lemma 4.4.1]
, we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{O_{F}}(\overline{\mathcal{E}},G)=i_{K}^{-1}(L).

By the definition of special cycles, for any
O_{F}
-lattice
M\subset\mathbb{V}
, we have
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M)(O_{K})
if and only if
M\subset i_{\bar{k}}(\operatorname{Hom}_{O_{F}}(\overline{\mathcal{E}},G))
. By (
5.1
),
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M)(O_{K})
if and only if

(5.2)

\displaystyle M\subset i_{\bar{k}}(i_{K}^{-1}(L)).

Now assume that
z\in\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})(O_{K})
corresponds to an
O_{F}
-hermitian module
G
of signature
(1,n-1)
over
O_{K}
. By (
5.2
), we have

L^{\flat}\subset i_{\bar{k}}(i_{K}^{-1}(L)).

Define
M^{\flat}\coloneqq L_{F}^{\flat}\cap i_{\bar{k}}(i_{K}^{-1}(L))
. By (
5.2
), we still have
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})(O_{K})
. Moreover, we have

M^{\flat}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}L\cap i_{K}(i_{\bar{k}}^{-1}(L_{F}^{\flat})).

Set
\mathbb{W}=i_{K}(i_{\bar{k}}^{-1}(L_{F}^{\flat}))
, which has the same dimension as
L_{F}^{\flat}
.

Definition 5.1
.

Define
H(\mathbb{V})
to be the collection of
O_{F}
-lattices
M^{\flat}\subset\mathbb{V}
of rank
n-1
such that

\displaystyle M^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h},(\pi)^{h-2},(\pi^{a}))\text{ with $a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}$}\quad\text{or}\quad M^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}(1^{n-h-2},(\pi)^{h},(\pi^{a}))\text{ with $a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$}.

Definition 5.2
.

For any lattice
L^{\prime\flat}
such that
L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime\flat}\subset(L^{\prime\flat})^{\vee}\subset L_{F}^{\flat}
, we define the primitive part
{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})^{\circ}
of the special cycle
{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})
inductively by setting

{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})^{\circ}\coloneqq{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\flat})-{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime\flat}\subset L^{\prime\prime\flat}\\
L^{\prime\prime\flat}\subset(L^{\prime\prime\flat})^{\vee}\subset L^{\prime\flat}_{F}\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\prime\prime\flat})^{\circ}.

Moreover, we define
\mathcal{Y}(L^{\prime\flat})^{\circ}
similarly.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
L^{\flat}\subseteq\mathbb{V}_{n}
be a hermitian
O_{F}
-lattice of rank
n-1
. Then

\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subseteq M^{\flat},\\
M^{\flat}\in H(\mathbb{V})\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{\circ}.

Moreover, we have the following.

(1)

If
M^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h},(\pi)^{h-2},(\pi^{a}))\text{ with $a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}$}
, then
\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}\cong\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})\simeq\mathcal{Z}(x)\subset\mathcal{N}_{2}^{[0]}
with
{\mathrm{val}}(x)=a-1,
which is a quasi-canonical lifting of degree
a-1
.

(2)

If
M^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}(1^{n-h-2},(\pi)^{h},(\pi^{a}))\text{ with $a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}_{\mathscr{H}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M^{\flat}\subset M_{1}\oplus N_{2}\subset\pi^{-1}M^{\flat}\\
N_{2}\approx(\pi^{-1})^{h}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(N_{2})^{\circ}.

Here, each
\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(N_{2})^{\circ}
is a quasi-canonical lifting of degree
a
. The summation index has cardinality:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}q^{2n-2}&\text{ if }a\geq 1,\\
q^{2n-2}\dfrac{1-(-q)^{-n}}{1-(-q)^{-1}}&\text{ if }a=0.\end{cases}

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
5.3
. We give the proof at the end of this section after some preparations.

First, by the similar method as in the appendix of
[
9
]
, we can prove the following two lemmas.

Lemma 5.4
.

Assume
L=L_{1}\obot L_{2}
where
L_{1}\approx(1)^{n-h}
and
L_{2}\approx(\pi)^{h}
. Let
x
be a primitive vector in
L
.

(1)

If
\mathrm{Pr}_{L_{1}}(x)
is primitive in
L_{1}
, then there exists
L_{1}^{\prime}\approx(1)^{n-h}
such that
x\in L_{1}^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
\mathrm{Pr}_{L_{1}}(x)
is not primitive in
L_{1}
, then there exists
L_{1}^{\prime}\approx(1)^{n-h}
and
L_{2}^{\prime}\approx(\pi)^{h}
such that
L=L_{1}^{\prime}\obot L_{2}^{\prime}
and
x\in L_{2}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Assume
L\approx(1)^{n-h}
or
(\pi)^{h}
. Then for any primitive vectors
x,x^{\prime}\in L
with
q(x)=q(x^{\prime})
, there exists
g\in\mathrm{U}(L)
such that
g(x)=x^{\prime}
.

With the help of the above two lemmas, we can prove the following.

Lemma 5.6
.

Assume
L
is a hermitian lattice represented by
\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h},(\pi)^{h})
and
\mathbb{W}\subset L_{F}
is a subspace of dimension
n-1
. Then
M^{\flat}\coloneqq\mathbb{W}\cap L
is represented either by
\mathrm{Diag}(1^{n-h-2},(\pi)^{h},(\pi^{a}))
with
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
or
\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h},(\pi)^{h-2},(\pi^{a}))
with
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. In the first case, we can write
M^{\flat}=M_{1}\obot M_{2}
such that
L=L_{1}\obot M_{2}
.

Proof.

First, we assume
M^{\flat}_{1}\coloneqq M^{\flat}\cap L_{1,F}
is not unimodular. Then the rank of
L_{1}
is at least 2 and we choose a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-h}\}
of
L_{1}
such that
e_{1},e_{2}\in L_{1}
and
(e_{1},e_{1})=(e_{2},e_{2})=0
,
(e_{1},e_{2})=1
and
(e_{i},e_{j})=0
for
i,j>2,i\neq j
. Since
M_{1}^{\flat}
is primitive in
L_{1}
but non-isometric to
L_{1}
, we know the rank of
M_{1}^{\flat}
is smaller than
n-h
. Hence the rank of
M_{1}^{\flat}
has to be
n-h-1
and
M_{2}^{\flat}\coloneqq M^{\flat}\cap L_{2,F}=L_{2}
. Now we choose a orthogonal basis
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-h-1}\}
of
M_{1}^{\flat}
, where we assume
q(x_{1})=(x_{1},x_{1})
has largest valuation among
\{q(x_{1}),\ldots,q(x_{n-h-1})\}
. In particular,
{\mathrm{val}}(q(x_{1}))>0
. By Lemmas
5.4
and
5.5
, we may assume
x_{1}=e_{1}+\frac{q(x_{1})}{2}e_{2}
. Since
M_{1}^{\flat}
is primitive, we can write
x_{i}=a_{i}(e_{1}-\frac{q(x_{1})}{2}e_{2})+\sum_{j=3}^{n-h}a_{ij}e_{j}
and for each
j
with
3\leq j\leq n-h
, there exists an
i
such that
a_{ij}
is a unit. Hence by possibly choosing a different basis
\{x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-h-1}\}
, we may assume
x_{i}=a_{i}(e_{1}-\frac{q(x_{1})}{2})e_{2})+e_{i+1}
. Now it is clear that
\mathrm{Span}\{x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-h-1}\}
is unimodular and
M^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h-2},(\pi)^{h},(\pi^{a}))
.

Now we assume
M_{1}^{\flat}\coloneqq M^{\flat}\cap L_{1,F}
is unimodular. If
\mathrm{rank}M_{1}^{\flat}=n-h-1
, then an argument as above shows that
M^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{n-h-1},(\pi)^{h})
. If If
\mathrm{rank}M_{1}^{\flat}=n-h
, then
\mathrm{rank}M_{2}^{\flat}=h-1
, and a similar argument as before shows that
M_{2}^{\flat}\approx(\pi)^{h-2}\obot(\pi^{a})
with
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then
M^{\flat}\approx(1)^{n-h}\obot(\pi)^{h-2}\obot(\pi^{a})
and
(1)^{n-h-1}\obot(\pi)^{h}
respectively.
∎

In particular, Lemma
5.6
implies that if
z\in\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})(O_{K})
, then
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})(O_{K})
where
M^{\flat}\in H(\mathbb{V})
.

Now we assume
z\in\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})(O_{K})
corresponds to a
M^{\flat}
represented by

(1)^{n-h-2}\obot(\pi^{a})\obot(\pi)^{h}
. By Proposition
2.7
,
we may reduce to the case
M^{\flat}
represented by

(\pi^{a})\obot(\pi)^{h}.

Proposition 5.7
.

Assume
z\in\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})(O_{K})
corresponds to a
M^{\flat}=M_{1}\obot M_{2}
, where
M_{1}
is represented by

(\pi^{a})
and
M_{2}
is represented by
(\pi)^{h}
. If
L=L_{1}\obot M_{2}
, then
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{2})^{\circ}.

Proof.

First, notice that
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
essentially by the definition of
M^{\flat}
. We may choose a basis
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
of
L_{1}
and a basis
\{x_{3},\cdots,x_{h+2}\}
of
M_{2}
such that the moment matrix of
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
and
\{x_{3},\cdots,x_{h+2}\}
is
(1)^{2}
and
(\pi)^{h}
. Let

\displaystyle L_{z^{\vee}}\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{O_{F}}(T_{p}\overline{\mathcal{E}},T_{p}G^{\vee}).

Composing
L
with
\lambda_{G}
, we obtain an embedding
L\hookrightarrow L_{z^{\vee}}
such that
L_{z^{\vee}}=\lambda_{G}(L_{1})\obot\pi^{-1}\lambda_{G}(M_{2}).
Moreover, the moment matrix of
\lambda_{G}(L_{1})\obot\pi^{-1}\lambda_{G}(M_{2})
is
(\pi)^{2}\obot(1)^{h}
. We can also directly check that
L_{z^{\vee}}\cap\lambda_{G}(i_{K}(i_{\bar{k}}^{-1}(L^{\flat}_{F})))=M_{1}\obot\pi^{-1}\lambda_{G}(M_{2})
. Hence,
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{2})^{\circ}
by the definition of special cycles.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Assume
M^{\flat}=M_{1}\obot M_{2}=M_{1}^{\prime}\oplus M_{2}
. Then

\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{2})^{\circ}=\mathcal{Z}(M_{1}^{\prime})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{2})^{\circ}.

Proof.

First of all, we have
\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{2})^{\circ}\subset\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})^{\circ}=\mathcal{Z}(M_{1}^{\prime})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})^{\circ}\subset\mathcal{Z}(M_{1}^{\prime})^{\circ}.
Hence,
\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{2})^{\circ}\subset\mathcal{Z}(M_{1}^{\prime})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{2})^{\circ}
. By switching the role of
M_{1}
and
M_{1}^{\prime}
, the lemma is proved.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
M^{\flat}=M_{1}\obot M_{2}
, where
M_{1}=\mathrm{span}\{x_{0}\}
, and
M_{2}
has a basis
\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{h}\}
with moment matrix
(\pi)^{h}
.
Assume
M^{\flat}\subset N^{\flat}\subset\pi^{-1}M^{\flat}
and
N^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}(\pi^{a},(\pi^{-1})^{h})
. Then
N^{\flat}=M_{1}\oplus\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}(x_{1}+\alpha_{1}x_{0}),\cdots,\pi^{-1}(x_{h}+\alpha_{h}x_{0})\}
, where
\alpha_{i}
is a representative of
O_{F}/(\pi)
.

Proof.

Since
M^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}(\pi^{a},(\pi)^{h})
and
N^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}(\pi^{a},(\pi^{-1})^{h})
,

N^{\flat}
must contain a sub-lattice of the form
\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}(x_{1}+\alpha_{1}x_{0}),\cdots,\pi^{-1}(x_{h}+\alpha_{h}x_{0})\}
.
Moreover, we have
M^{\flat}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\subset}}N^{\flat}\subset\pi^{-1}M^{\flat}
.

Notice that
M^{\flat}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\subset}}M_{1}\oplus\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}(x_{1}+\alpha_{1}x_{0}),\cdots,\pi^{-1}(x_{h}+\alpha_{h}x_{0})\}\subset N^{\flat}
. Hence
N^{\flat}=M_{1}\oplus\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}(x_{1}+\alpha_{1}x_{0}),\cdots,\pi^{-1}(x_{h}+\alpha_{h}x_{0})\}
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Assume
M^{\flat}=M_{1}\obot M_{2}
with basis
\{x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{h}\}
as in Lemma
5.9
. Let
N^{\flat}
and
(N^{\flat})^{\prime}
be two different lattices such that
M^{\flat}\subset N^{\flat}\subset\pi^{-1}M^{\flat}
,
M^{\flat}\subset(N^{\flat})^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}M^{\flat}
, and
N^{\flat}\approx(N^{\flat})^{\prime}\approx\mathrm{Diag}(\pi^{a},(\pi^{-1})^{h}).
Write
N^{\flat}=M_{1}\oplus N_{2}
and
(N^{\flat})^{\prime}=M_{1}\oplus N_{2}^{\prime}
. Then

\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(N_{2})^{\circ}(O_{K})\neq\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(N_{2}^{\prime})(O_{K}).

Proof.

Let
N^{\flat}=M_{1}\oplus\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}(x_{1}+\alpha_{1}x_{0}),\cdots,\pi^{-1}(x_{h}+\alpha_{h}x_{0})\}
and
(N^{\flat})^{\prime}=M_{1}\oplus\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}(x_{1}+\alpha_{1}^{\prime}x_{0}),\cdots,\pi^{-1}(x_{h}+\alpha_{h}^{\prime}x_{0})\}
where
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}
are representatives of
O_{F}/(\pi)
. If
\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(N_{2})^{\circ}(O_{K})=\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(N_{2}^{\prime})(O_{K}),
then we have nontrivial
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(N_{2})^{\circ}(O_{K})\cap\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(N_{2}^{\prime})(O_{K})
. This implies that
z\in\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{1})
. In particular,
z\in\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{1})\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}x_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-1}x_{h}\})(O_{K}).
However,
notice that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{1})\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}x_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-1}x_{h}\})\cong\mathcal{Z}(\pi^{-1}M_{1})\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}x_{1},\cdots,\pi^{-1}x_{h}\})

by cancellation law and the fact that
\mathcal{Z}(\pi^{-1}M_{1})=\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{1})
in
\mathcal{N}_{2}
. This contradicts the fact
z\not\in\mathcal{Z}(\pi^{-1}M_{1})^{\circ}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

Assume
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}(O_{K})
. By Lemma
5.6
, we know that
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})(O_{K})
where
M^{\flat}\in H(\mathbb{V})
.

If
M^{\flat}=M_{1}\obot M_{2}
, where
M_{1}\approx(1)^{n-h}
and
M_{2}\approx\mathrm{Diag}((\pi)^{h-2},(\pi^{a}))
with
a>0
, then according to Proposition
2.7
,
\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})\cong\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})\subset\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[h]}
. Then applying the duality between
\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[h]}
and
\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[0]}
, we have
\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})\subset\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[h]}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{Z}(M_{2}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{N}_{h}^{[0]}
, where
M_{2}^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Diag}((1)^{h-2},(\pi^{a-1}))
. Then by Proposition
2.7
and
[
13
, Proposition 8.1]
,
\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
is isomorphic to a quasi-canonical lifting of degree
a-1
.

Now we assume
M^{\flat}\approx\mathrm{Diag}(1^{n-h-2},(\pi)^{h},(\pi^{a}))\text{ with $a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$}.
Then according to Proposition
5.7
, and Lemma
5.9
, we have
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(N_{2})^{\circ}(O_{K})
for some
N_{2}
, where
N_{2}=\mathrm{span}\{\pi^{-1}(x_{1}+\alpha_{1}x_{0}),\cdots,\pi^{-1}(x_{h}+\alpha_{h}x_{0})\}
and each
\alpha_{i}
is some representative of
O_{F}/(\pi)
. If
M_{1}\cong\mathrm{Diag}(1^{n-h-2},\pi^{a})
and
M_{2}\cong(\pi)^{h}
, then by Proposition
2.7
and
[
13
, Proposition 8.1]
again,
\mathcal{Z}(M_{1})^{\circ}\cdot\mathcal{Y}(\pi^{-1}M_{2})^{\circ}
is isomorphic to a quasi-canonical lifting of degree
a
.

According to the above discussion, we have

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(L^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subseteq M^{\flat},\\
M^{\flat}\in H(\mathbb{V})\end{subarray}}m(M^{\flat})\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{\circ},

and
\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{\circ}
has the desired description.

Now we show
m(M^{\flat})=1
for any
M^{\flat}
in the above identity following the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 4.2.1]
closely. It suffices to show
O_{K}[\epsilon]
-points of both sides of (
5.3
) are the same where
\epsilon^{2}=0
. First, each
O_{K}
-point of the right hand side of (
5.3
) has a unique lift to an
O_{K}[\epsilon]
-point. Therefore we only need to show each
z\in\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{\circ}(O_{K})
has a unique lift in
\mathcal{Z}(M^{\flat})_{\mathscr{H}}^{\circ}(O_{K}[\epsilon])
.

Let
G
be the corresponding
O_{F}
-hermitian module of signature
(1,n-1)
over
O_{K}
and
\mathbb{D}(G)
be the (covariant)
O_{F_{0}}
-relative Dieudonné crystal of
G
. First, we have an action
O_{F}\otimes_{O_{F_{0}}}O_{K}\simeq O_{K}\oplus O_{K}
on
\mathbb{D}(G)(O_{K})
induced by the action of
O_{F}
via
